15.92073pt\raise-9.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 75.67361pt\raise-9.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}.

This canonical map is the top arrow in
the diagram (
5.61
).
This exhibits our Hilbert space

{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega_{1}},
which was the Hilbert space
from the GNS construction associated
to the EPR density matrix
\rho_{1}
for observer
\mathcal{O}_{1},

as a vector subspace of our original Hilbert
space
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{1}\otimes{{\mathcal{H}}}_{2}

for the entangled EPR vector
|\Psi\rangle.

Note that the map (
6.22
)
is surjective because

(6.23)

\pi(a\otimes\mathds{1})|\Psi\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Big(a_{\uparrow\uparrow}|\!\uparrow\downarrow\rangle+a_{\downarrow\uparrow}|\!\downarrow\downarrow\rangle-a_{\uparrow\downarrow}|\!\uparrow\uparrow\rangle-a_{\downarrow\downarrow}|\!\downarrow\uparrow\rangle\Big).

It is also an isometry by Lemma
5.9
because

(6.24)

\lVert\pi(a\otimes\mathds{1})|\Psi\rangle\rVert^{2}=\langle\Psi|\pi(a^{{\dagger}}\otimes\mathds{1})\pi(a\otimes\mathds{1})|\Psi\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j,k\in\{\uparrow,\downarrow\}}\!|a_{jk}|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}{\rm tr}(a^{{\dagger}}a)=\lVert a\rVert_{\omega_{1}}^{2}

for all
a\in{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega_{1}}.

Hence, (
6.22
) is a unitary intertwiner.

Appendix A

In the GNS construction, we use
oplax-natural transformations,
which are different from the
pseudo-natural transformations
that appear in the early literature on 2-categories

13
]
.
Fortunately, the difference is minor.
For completeness, we include this definition
along with the notion of modifications

]
.

The definition of a modification does not
change if one uses oplax-natural
transformations instead of
pseudo-natural transformations.

Definition A.10
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be two
2-categories,
F,G:\mathcal{C}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{D}

be two 2-functors, and
\rho,\sigma:F\Rightarrow G

be two oplax-natural transformations. A

modification

m

from
\sigma
to
\rho
, written as

m:\sigma\Rrightarrow\rho
and drawn as

(A.11)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 78.81651pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.61111pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern for every 1-morphism

(A.13)

\textstyle{\sigma(y)\circ F(\alpha)}

\textstyle{\rho(y)\circ F(\alpha)}
\textstyle{G(\alpha)\circ\rho(x)}
\scriptstyle{\sigma(\alpha)}

\scriptstyle{\rho(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{m(y)\circ\mathrm{id}_{F(\alpha)}}

commutes, i.e.

Compositions of oplax-natural transformations
and modifications are not changed as a result
of these alterations to the usual definitions.
In particular, the vertical composition of oplax-natural
transformations is denoted using vertical concatenation as in

(A.15)

and is defined by the assignments

(A.16)

\textstyle{x}
\textstyle{F(x)}
\textstyle{G(x)}
\textstyle{H(x)}
\scriptstyle{\rho(x)}
\scriptstyle{\sigma(x)}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}\rho\\
\sigma\end{smallmatrix}}

for each object
x
in
\mathcal{C}
and

(A.17)

\textstyle{x}
\textstyle{y}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{F(y)}
\textstyle{G(y)}
\textstyle{H(y)}
\textstyle{F(x)}
\textstyle{G(x)}
\textstyle{H(x)}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{F(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\rho(x)}
\scriptstyle{\rho(y)}
\scriptstyle{G(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\rho(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\sigma(x)}
\scriptstyle{\sigma(y)}
\scriptstyle{H(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\sigma(\alpha)}

for each morphism
y\xleftarrow{\alpha}x
in
\mathcal{C}.

Similarly, the vertical composition of modifications is
denoted using vertical concatenation as in

(A.18)

.

Conditions (
A.23
) and
(
A.24
) are known as the

zig-zag identities
.
An adjunction as above is typically
written as a quadruple
(f,g,\eta,\epsilon)

and we say
f
is
left adjoint

to
g
and write
f\dashv g.

A left adjoint is unique in the following sense.

Lemma A.25
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a (strict) 2-category and let

x
and
y
be two objects in
\mathcal{C}.

Let
x\xleftarrow{g}y
be a 1-morphism
and let

(f,g,\eta,\epsilon)
and

(f^{\prime},g,\eta^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime})
be adjunctions
in which which
f
and
f^{\prime}
are both
left adjoint to
g.

Then there exists a vertically invertible
2-morphism

\sigma:f\Rightarrow f^{\prime}
such that

In this paper, we focus on an adjunction
in a particular 2-category obtained from
functors between 2-categories.

Definition A.28
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be two (strict)
2-categories. Let

\mathbf{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})

be the 2-category whose objects
are (strict) functors from

\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{D},
1-morphisms are
oplax-natural transformations,
and 2-morphisms are modifications.

We spell out what it means to have
an adjunction in this 2-category
explicitly.

Lemma A.29
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be two
(strict)
2-categories and let

\mathbf{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})

be the functor 2-category described in
Definition (
A.28
).
An adjunction in
\mathbf{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})

consists of two (strict) functors

F,G:\mathcal{C}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{D},

two oplax-natural transformations

\sigma:F\Rightarrow G
and
\rho:G\Rightarrow F,

and two modifications

\eta:\mathrm{id}_{F}\Rrightarrow\begin{smallmatrix}\sigma\\
\rho\end{smallmatrix}

and

\epsilon:\begin{smallmatrix}\rho\\
\sigma\end{smallmatrix}\Rrightarrow\mathrm{id}_{G}

such that the diagrams

Remark A.32
.

Because the zig-zag identities only involve
the equality of modifications, and since
the datum of a modification
consists only of an assignment of
2-morphisms in
\mathcal{D}
to objects
of
\mathcal{C},
they can be re-expressed as

(A.33)

for every object
x
of
\mathcal{C},
i.e. for every object
x
in
\mathcal{C},

the quadruple
\big(\sigma(x),\rho(x),\eta(x),\epsilon(x)\big)

is an adjunction.
